We welcome new bloggers -- contact us for details . <h> Jessica Bell 's blog <p> Jessica Bell is a lecturer on advocacy and government relations at Ryerson University , and a member of the Ruckus Society , and Tools for Change . <h> KI Nation to paddle to protect their watershed <p> From August 24 to September 7 , a team of paddlers from the KI Indigenous Nation will venture 400 km beyond the nearest road to travel the ancient trading route from their remote fly-in community to Hudson 's Bay . <p> They are calling on Ontario to respect their deep connection to the wild Fawn River watershed -- a foundation of their culture , and the threatened heart of the world 's largest intact forest . <p> " The KI people have protected our entire home watershed through Indigenous Law , " said KI Chief Donny Morris . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ protection before this sacred landscape is poisoned by the diamond , gold , and metals mining companies who have set their sights on it . " <p> En route they will use satellites to transmit blogs , photos , and audio to thousands of supporters via social media portals as they share the landscape with threatened woodland caribou , wolves , sturgeon , polar bears , beluga whales and the iconic northern lights . Follow their journey at www.kilands.org . <p> Through bold action campaigns KI and their supporters have stopped mining companies Platinex and God 's Lake Resources from exploring on their land . The community has also successfully pressured the Ontario government to withdraw approximately half of their watershed from all mining activity . <p> But Ontario has yet to recognize KI 's right to protect their entire watershed of 13,025 square kms and to govern their Homeland . Much of the community 's watershed remains open to speculation by gold , diamond , and metals mining companies seeking to capitalize on Ontario 's mining boom . <p> Indigenous communities like KI depend on the clean water and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to safeguard their water . Last year the KI community voted overwhelmingly in favour of the KI Watershed Declaration , which places the entire 13,025 square km of their vast intact watershed off limits to industry under KI 's Indigenous Law . <p> The Boreal forest and wetlands of KI Homeland is part of the world 's largest carbon storehouse on land -- a critical buffer against runaway climate change . The Boreal is also the world 's greatest reservoir of fresh water , and is among the largest unlogged forests left on the planet . Some of the greatest wild rivers in the world flow through territories of Indigenous Nations in Ontario 's Far North , each running free for many hundreds of km without any dams or diversions . Their clean source waters are filtered by the mossy forest and wetlands of extensive pristine watersheds . 
@@1083173 <p> According to Bill McKibben , environmentalist and author of The End of Nature , " May was the warmest on record for the Northern Hemisphere -- the 327th consecutive month in which the temperature of the entire globe exceeded the 20th-century average . " The odds of this happening by chance are greater than the number of stars in the universe . <p> June broke or tied 3,215 high-temperature records across the U.S. July was the hottest month in the U.S. since they began keeping records in 1895 . <p> Around the world the force and frequency of severe weather is increasing . Fires , floods , tornadoes and heat waves are taking thousands of lives and causing untold damage . <p> There is both good news and bad news about climate change in Canada . <p> The good news : More Canadians than ever believe in climate change and they want their governments to take action . The bad news : Governments continue to act as if job creation and a cleaner environment are conflicting goals . <p> In 2010 , a series of public opinion @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ warming to be real and caused by emissions from vehicles and industrial facilities . Another 17 per cent believed in global warming , but felt it was caused by nature . The remaining 25 per cent either did n't believe in climate change or had no opinion . <p> A more recent poll conducted by Leger Marketing on behalf of the Public Policy Forum shows that Canadians are increasingly concerned about climate change : <p> * 80 per cent believe there is solid evidence of global warming ; 73 per cent think it 's a serious problem . <p> * A majority believe that all three levels of government should be taking more action to reduce global warming . <p> * A majority support policy options like cap-and-trade and carbon taxes . More significant , they support them even if it means up to $50 a month in additional energy expense . <p> Given this level of belief , concern and willingness to sacrifice financially , why has n't the federal government given more leadership to emission reduction ? <p> The Conservatives do n't seem to believe it 's possible to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . One has to trump the other . And since the economy remains the No. 1 concern , they feel justified in pursuing economic growth at the expense of the environment . <p> NRTEE has laid out a long-term , science-based , low-carbon " climate prosperity " growth plan . The Conservatives silenced the plan and its scientists . Why ? Because according to Foreign Affairs Minister John Baird , " promoting a carbon tax is something that the people of Canada have repeatedly rejected . " <p> Maybe a majority of Conservatives still reject low-carbon policies . But a majority of Canadians now support them even if it means paying higher energy bills . <p> This month the Harper government bowed to Alberta on emission rules . It will allow Alberta to regulate its own emissions on a per-barrel basis , rather than impose a cap on the sector . This sets the stage for a doubling of oilsands production . <p> Federal Environment Minister Peter Kent said he has " not set a target " for the oil and gas industry . " It 's a best efforts approach @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ineffective " and " far too weak . " <p> A 2009 study commissioned by the Pembina Institute found that employment would grow slightly faster in Canada under more extensive climate policy scenarios than with business as usual . It 's not surprising . <p> Germany , Europe 's economic powerhouse , is making it happen . One sunny day in May , this Northern Hemisphere country generated half of its power from solar panels and another 10 per cent from wind turbines . <p> Long-term government support for low-carbon energy has paid off . More than 350,000 workers are employed in the renewable energy sector . Germany has earned a 30 per cent share of the world 's wind turbine market . It exports $16 billion in renewable energy technology annually . <p> Germany is reducing pollution and creating jobs at the same time . A majority of Canadians want the same thing . The Conservatives are ignoring the climate prosperity option at their political peril .

@@1083290 <h> Environmental justice for Lake Ainslie <p> Robert Parkins of the Margaree Environmental Association points out the area at the centre of an appeal to the Nova Scotia Supreme Court during a presentation June 26 in Halifax . ( CHRISTIAN LAFORCE / Staff / File ) <p> The question is not whether to call some water a " brook " or " a drainage ditch . " The question is whether we want government by wealth or government by thought . The definition of a watercourse ( the lack of definition , actually ) turned the tide on the Nova Scotia Supreme Court 's recent ruling in the case of Cape Breton community groups fighting an oil well . As the Margaree Environmental Association ( MEA ) points out , the Environment Act is impotent , and the rights to which local citizens have recourse are flimsy . <p> The judiciary process prevented MEA from bringing forward new evidence regarding the waterway in question because this issue arose at the appeal stage . Thanks to the MEA 's efforts in court , there is growing concern that the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ainslie runs roughshod over local resources and people . <p> The exploratory well would open the door to any number of oil wells for both traditional drilling and hydraulic fracturing . Though fracking is n't on the lips of the government or the oil corporation yet , PetroWorth president Neal Mednick indirectly argued in favour of its use in Nova Scotia ; he was quoted in October 2010 in the media as saying people need to " understand what the truth is , as opposed to all of the unfounded fears . " We can only presume he knows better than the Environment Canada Chemicals Management Plan , which found over half of the chemicals in fracking fluid to be of potential harm to human health . PetroWorth 's agenda is to make the discovery and sell the lease to a partner corporation . If that partner decides on fracking , there will be a permit request for fracking . Heaven knows we do n't trust the environment minister to protect us from that possibility . <p> The court 's ruling on Aug. 3 rested on the inadequacy of our existing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ regulations to follow , the decision to grant the permit was up to his discretion . Belliveau 's counsel argued that the Environment Act mandates protection of the environment in the context of diverse goals , including those of economic development : " Many interests arise and must be balanced in the context of each decision of the minister or his delegate . " The court ruling grants this , which means that the sole point of contention in court was the " watercourse . " <p> On this count , the ruling states , " The minister 's interpretation of the definition of ' watercourse ' would survive on a standard of reasonableness or a standard of correctness . " The onus to make a specific demarcation of " watercourse " rested with the appellant , and the Environment Act contained no strict provision to back up MEA 's reasonable belief that a brook running through the drilling site and leading to Lake Ainslie is indeed a watercourse . <p> The communities around Lake Ainslie and the Margaree River oppose the oil well on compelling ground . Local resident Robert @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The brook through the drilling site carries water from a wetland to Lake Ainslie . The adjacent Margaree River is a heritage river , providing recreational fishing that attracts many visitors to the region . The Margaree Salmon Association , the Lake Aislie and Judique Development Associations and First Nations have all expressed opposition to the oil permit . <p> Even if one were to find sufficient benefit in the PetroWorth proposal to justify the potential environmental , economic and cultural harm , how is it that Nova Scotia can abide by such ineffective environmental regulations ? If the government is willing to compromise our natural systems , our cultural heritage and our input in decision-making , we need to make the case for laws that constrain these brutal political affronts . Let 's start right now with an Environment Act that protects the environment before profits , and specifies adequate setbacks of all industrial processes from homes , waterways and vital resources . <p> The act should include requirements for meaningful public participation so that community groups are n't left stranded in uphill legal battles when they have criticisms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , should n't the environment minister have a duty to consider the impacts to climate change of industrial proposals like this ? Clearly , that did n't have an iota of importance when the minister balanced environment and oil exploration . All this adds up to one conclusion : We need an Environment Act that acts like one . <p> John Bennett is executive director , Sierra Club of Canada . Cate May Burton is the summer researcher at Sierra Club of Canada , Atlantic Chapter . @@1083415 <p> In Canada 's Far North , where two litres of milk can cost $14 , a bag of flour $33 , and 10 pieces of fried chicken $61.99 , the government of Nunavut thinks a better future might lie in the past . So it has launched a program encouraging residents to follow the example of their ancestors and live off the land , harvesting more traditional " country food " like seal , muskox and even ground squirrel . " It 's partly for reasons of cost , and it 's partly for reasons of nutrition , " says Ed McKenna , director of the territory 's Anti-Poverty Secretariat . " But it 's also related to culture . For many people it 's their preferred food . " <p> And more to the point , it 's a straightforward solution to one of Nunavut 's most persistent social ills : hunger . A 2010 McGill University study published in the Canadian Medical Association Journal estimated that nearly 70 per cent of preschoolers in the territory live in " food insecure " households , where there @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to eat . Another survey , undertaken by the federal government , found that half of 11- to 15-year-olds in Nunavut reported sometimes going hungry . " The numbers are pretty stark , " says McKenna . " It 's a major issue . " <p> The Country Food Distribution Program is providing close to $4 million in funding over three years to help isolated municipalities feed themselves . Grants are available to help establish or upgrade community freezers , or set up local fresh-kill markets . But so far , the most popular aspect of the plan has been the direct subsidies -- up to $10,000 -- for large-scale hunts . Last year , 14 of the territory 's 25 settlements took advantage of the cash , which is earmarked for basic supplies . " Harvesting has become more dependent on Ski-Doos , so you 're talking about the gas , as well as the cost of firearms and bullets , and then food and other equipment , " says McKenna . " It 's the kind of expense that 's beyond the reach of many , many people now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 900 at the far northwestern edge of Hudson 's Bay , received a grant last winter and organized a hunt that bagged 20 caribou for distribution to those in need . " We 're a small community and there 's hardly any work here , " says Michel Akkuardkuk , president of the Arviq Hunters and Trappers Association . " We wanted to give country food to some of the elders and people who do n't have snowmobiles . " The group is applying for another subsidy this year . " It 's a good program , " says Akkuardkuk . " It helps . " <p> The shift toward a more traditional Arctic diet is already well under way in Nunavut 's capital , Iqaluit , where a community group established a country food market in 2010 . The monthly open-air sales are held in a park next to the local supermarket , allowing hunters to drive right up on their snowmobiles or ATVs with trailers full of meat or fish . " It all sells out in about 10 minutes , " says Willie Hyndman , the executive director @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's the middle of a blizzard , there are always about 150 people waiting . " A medium-sized Arctic char goes for $20 ; so too for a large freezer bag of seal meat . Wild berries can be had for $10 a container . Walrus , ptarmigan , seaweed and other delicacies are also for sale . Elders are given first crack , but the market is open to everyone . <p> It 's been such a success that the obvious question is why no one has tried it before . Hyndman says many people assured him the plan would n't work , citing government regulations , or opposition from hunters and trappers . But as it turned out , the roadblocks were more imagined than real . <p> The market 's popularity may also have something to do with simmering anger in the North about the ever-increasing cost of packaged goods . Earlier this summer , a Facebook group devoted to cataloguing examples of price gouging -- $20 cabbages and $105 cases of bottled water -- attracted more than 10,000 followers and ended up sparking demonstrations outside supermarkets @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ food that 's available up here . And it 's a lot cheaper , " says Hyndman . <p> There is some concern , however , that encouraging more hunting will harm species that are already under stress from climate change , habitat destruction or historical overharvesting . A recent report by Nunavut 's Environment Department cautioned that caribou herds are already in a long-term decline , and that not enough is known about the sustainability of other large-game options like reindeer . The key recommendation was to try and further diversify the local diet , pointing people toward some less traditional fare like Arctic hare , snow geese and squirrel . <p> McKenna says the government is mindful of the need to avoid creating new problems as they search for solutions to existing challenges . " We do n't want to be encouraging people to do something that 's not going to be in their benefit in the long run . " More studies will be undertaken , and there are no plans to commercialize the hunts and start exporting fish and game outside the territory . " The focus @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's not a large program , but it can have a pretty good impact . @@1085204 <h> Hoola Hoops , Classic Cars , Car Hops Part of A&amp;W Fundraiser <h> You are here <p> On August 23 , if you happen to be cruisin ' by one of the more than 750 A&amp;W restaurants across Canada and see a bit of ' 50s throwback hoopla going on -- hoola hoops , classic cars , car hops -- it 's because the retail food chain is holding a nationwide fundraiser for the Multiple Sclerosis Society of Canada called Cruisin ' For A Cause . <p> For every Teen Burger sold that day , the company will donate $1 . There will also be $1 paper cutouts in the shape of a car , a $2 Cruisin ' For A Cause fridge magnet , and donation boxes on-site . Online , people can give via **27;0;TOOLONG . <p> To help bring attention to the day and lure customers , each restaurant will host a special event , which have been known to include classic car gatherings , retro music , car hop service , dunk tanks , A&amp;W beer chugging contests and visits from the Great @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ MS . Crusin ' For A Cause launched in 2009 , A&amp;W Food Services of Canada has raised more than $2 million to support people living with MS and MS research in Canada . Last year 's event alone raised more than $1 million . <p> " We are thrilled by the $2 million raised so far and are confident we can work together to build on this achievement by making this year 's Cruisin ' for a Cause Day bigger and better than ever , " Paul Hollands , president/CEO of A&amp;W Food Services of Canada Inc. said in a statement . <p> Multiple sclerosis is a chronic , often disabling disease of the brain and spinal cord . It is the most common neurological disease of young adults in Canada . Most people with MS are diagnosed between the ages of 15 and 40 , and the unpredictable effects of MS last for the rest of their lives . @@1085226 <h> The 401 -- ing of the Maritimes <p> Highway 401 stretches more than 800 kilometres east-west across Ontario , ranging from four lanes to the multi-lane collector-express system in and around Toronto . ( THE CANADIAN PRESS ) <p> Once upon a time , New Brunswick liked to think of itself as the picture-book province , and with some justification . Every corner in the road revealed another little swale of small farms , rolling hills , cows and green fields . It 's now known as the drive-through province . The small farms have disappeared into mono-crop exercises for frozen food magnates , and the little swales have been paved over . <p> You can now travel through three Canadian provinces , from the St. Lawrence all the way to Halifax , and see nothing but the same concrete and asphalt vistas that you can while driving from Toronto to Montreal on the 401 . Progress has arrived in Eastern Canada . <p> Coming into Nova Scotia , you can drive happily on four lanes from Sackville , N.B. , to Halifax without touching down anywhere @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can find a station . The province of Quebec is busy filling in the last two-lane gaps between the St. Lawrence and the New Brunswick border and , in the process , losing little valleys in the same sea of divided highways and access ramps that its Maritime cousins have already constructed . <p> It 's difficult to pick a favourite but if I had to , it would be the divided highway from Halifax to the town of Windsor . Each side is wide enough to land the largest planes , and there 's a scenic decaying railroad line abutting one roadside . The grandeur of this particular highway takes the breath away . When did rural roads turn into engineering exercises in pyramid building ? What do these cement monstrosities have to do with the lives of ordinary people and communities ? <p> You can see why it happened around Toronto and Montreal . These are multi-million human beehives where governments have refused to invest in shared transit -- but people need to get around , so they just kept paving over stuff . Last time I looked , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> But East Coast Canada ? The population of Nova Scotia is smaller than the population of the city of Ottawa . Ditto New Brunswick . These provinces are small places serving small towns and rural populations , but have the same philosophy of " got ta move that traffic ! " <p> The Cabot Trail , where I live , has survived these modern-day pyramid builders . It still has two lanes and is much loved by tourists from around the world , not just for the scenery but because it still goes through small villages . The road still feels like it has a place as well as a destination . You can get out of the car and walk around in a place that it is more than a gas station . <p> But even here , the engineers ca n't imagine that a road should actually serve the community as well as the machines passing through . There are no paved shoulders . Nowhere for cyclists to cycle safely or pedestrians and joggers to move . The road ends in a white line at the edge of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ forced to risk their lives or bump along gravel -- and sometimes even this is impossible because there is nothing but a ditch . <p> I am an old guy now , but I 've been coming to Cape Breton since I was nothing more than a grandson looking up and out at the world around me . Now , I have a different view . It tends to be less immediate , more across the years than in them . If you missed the seriousness of my complaints , let me be crystal clear . Basing the life of cities , countryside and provinces around divided highways is destructive , and it 's destructive in many ways . It reduces the quality of life for all who live near them . They are dangerous contributors to climate change and to a worldwide economy that is neither fair nor sustainable . Walking sticks made in China , but purchased in the village of Cheticamp for walking along local Highland trails : What sense is there in that ? <p> Surely , we should be making our own walking sticks . Surely @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ well as those passing by ? But we do n't . We are all sitting on the end of these cement and asphalt umbilical cords that truck milk from " production centres " and walking sticks from China . The culture of Cape Breton endured for several centuries and is still used today to attract tourists . The modern one wo n't . <p> Clive Doucet is a writer and former Ottawa city councillor . He is the author of My Grandfather 's Cape Breton ; his most recent book is Urban Meltdown : Cities , Climate Change and Politics as Usual . <p> My Grandfather loved to drive around the country backroads of Pictou County . @@1085592 <p> WASHINGTON -- Rare wildfires sparked by lightning near Hudson Bay are threatening the habitat of polar bears , encroaching on the old tree roots and frozen soil where females make their dens and give birth to cubs . <p> Polar bears are more typically threatened by melting ice , which they use as platforms for hunting seals , but those who live near Hudson Bay spend their summers resting up on shore when the bay thaws , according to Steven Amstrup , a former polar bear specialist with the U.S. Geological Survey and now chief scientist at the non-profit conservation organization , Polar Bears International . <p> " It 's a cool , wet environment that does n't burn very often , " Amstrup said by telephone from Washington state . " It 's not an environment where the forest is adapted to fires very much . " <p> High temperatures this week in Churchill , Man . ranged from 15 to 18 C , with overnight lows above freezing . The U.S. National Snow and Ice Data Center indicated in a map of Arctic ice cover @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is ice-free . <p> Female polar bears in the western Hudson Bay population use dens under the root crowns of small spruce trees that grow slowly in permafrost soils . Some dens have been used for over 100 years . <p> " Not only is the permafrost no longer permanent , tree roots needed to stabilize the den structure are disappearing , " Amstrup said . " The kinds of habitats where mother polar bears in this area give birth to their cubs are simply disappearing as the world warms . " <p> When trees burn , as some may this summer , the roots die , further damaging bear dens , he said . <p> Unlike other polar bear populations , where pregnant females use dens dug in snow , Hudson Bay females come ashore to these tree-root dens to rest and give birth , remaining in the dens until the following spring . <p> " They 're essentially food-deprived in the summertime . They come ashore and basically just rest and try to save energy . They crawl into these dens , it 's cool in there , they @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ until the snow comes and until they give birth , " Amstrup said . <p> Without these dens , he said , females would not be able to conserve their energy . And if snow comes late , cubs could be born out in the open , where they would be exceedingly vulnerable . <p> " Cubs are born at about a pound and a half ( 680 grams ) , blind , nearly hairless , essentially immobile and totally helpless , " he said in an email . " Their survival depends upon the shelter of the den to protect them from the elements . " <p> Polar bears have government protection in Canada and the United States . The U.S. polar bear population in Alaska is listed as threatened under the Endangered Species Act because of increasing damage to their icy habitat by climate change .

@@1087480 <p> In the city that inspired " The Maple Leaf Forever , " the maple leaf may not be for long . <p> Of course , nobody knows for sure what will happen to Toronto 's much-celebrated tree canopy -- a lush urban forest of more than 10 million trees -- as climate change creeps upon it , along with the pests brought by warmer weather . <p> Of the 116 different species growing in Toronto , maples make up about a third of them , city statistics show . But for how much longer ? <p> " I do n't think we really know how climate change is going to affect our native tree species , " says Janet McKay , executive director and founder of Local Enhancement and Appreciation of Forests ( LEAF ) , a non-profit that subsidizes the planting of trees and vegetation on private property in Toronto and York Region . <p> " These are species that would be at the northern edge of their range here in Toronto . But in downtown Toronto you have the heat-island effect , so these species @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increase , the range where we can plant those will begin to shift north . " <p> Trees take up the carbon dioxide spewed by our cars and exhale oxygen . At the same time , they sweep the air of pollution . They suck up rainwater and keep it from taxing our storm drains . Their shade helps reduce air temperature , cuts our air-conditioning costs and provides shelter from the sun . They give our wildlife food and habitat . Even the sound of a breeze rustling through the leaves makes us feel cooler . <p> In other words , trees are not only good at staving off climate change , they 're also good at helping us adapt to it . <p> Befall us it will , say the experts . And trees are as vulnerable to heat waves , torrential downpours , ice storms and high winds as our electricity transmission lines and bridges and roads . <p> " We are trying to increase the canopy , " says Beth McEwen , city manager of urban forest renewal . " The difficulty is , with extreme weather @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only the trees planted this year but also the trees that have been planted in the last few years . " <p> A powerful windstorm is all it takes for a stressed tree to come crashing down -- on cars , homes , people . Downed trees block culverts , causing floods . They knock out power lines . They block streets , impeding emergency vehicles . <p> If we do n't take care of our trees , they ca n't take care of us . <p> Both LEAF and the city are working to educate the public on the benefits of trees . <p> " We definitely saw more drought impacts of young trees dying , so we were trying to promote more watering of boulevard trees , " says McEwen . <p> But citizens also need to take responsibility for public trees , as the city ca n't keep up with ensuring that trees in parks get enough water . " <p> Drought is not the only threat . " <p> " At this point , none of the researchers has said stop planting this , or that wo n't survive , " adds McKay . " I do n't think anybody knows how the changes will play out . @@1087631 <p> A few years back , this was one of those vague and fungible buzzwords popular in management circles . <p> Describing any metric as " directionally " positive suggested you were making progress toward a laudable target . But what it really meant was that you were only stumbling in a certain direction rather than actually racing there with purpose . <p> Once you scrubbed the term with a wire brush , " directionally " was really a cover for failure . <p> That 's probably the fairest assessment of Toronto 's efforts in dealing with the transportation challenges posed by climate change . <p> We 've developed some bold plans , yet politics and the usual funding conundrums have brought about so many speed bumps that progress has been painfully slow . <p> " We 're moving at a snail 's pace and we ca n't afford to be moving at a snail 's pace , " says Franz Hartmann , executive director of the Toronto Environmental Alliance . <p> Transportation is not just the greatest single contributor to greenhouse-gas emissions -- at nearly a third @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ growing . <p> Since 70 per cent of the transportation sector 's emissions stem from personal travel , public transit alternatives are key if we hope to move people around the region with the smallest possible carbon footprint . <p> And the pressure to do so will only increase amid the swelling populations of Durham , York and Halton . <p> " This is where the growth is going to be , " says Burda . The core of Toronto , for instance , was n't laid out with automobiles in mind , since they did n't yet exist . <p> The key to bolstering transit and thereby tackling climate change , in other words , is not to dismiss any option out of hand . <p> " Everything needs to be out on the table , " says Hartmann . @@1087659 <p> rabble blogs are the personal pages of some of Canada 's most insightful progressive activists and commentators . All opinions belong to the writer ; however , writers are expected to adhere to our guidelines . We welcome new bloggers -- contact us for details . <h> Behind The Numbers <p> Behind The Numbers delivers timely , progressive commentary on issues that affect Canadians , including the economy , poverty , inequality , climate change , budgets , taxes , public services , employment and much more . Contributors include staff and research associates from the Canadian Centre for Policy Alternatives ( CCPA ) . The views expressed on this blog are those of the individual contributors , and do not necessarily represent the views of the CCPA . Visit the blog at Behindthenumbers.ca . <h> Canada 's economic problem is not high wages <p> Bill Curry reports in the Globethat , at last year 's economic policy retreat , business leaders urged Finance Minister Flaherty to reduce the pay of " overpriced " Canadian workers , including through anti-union " right to work " legislation . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ budget introduced new rules which will require most EI claimants to accept jobs at much lower wages , and will allow employers of temporary foreign workers to pay less than the prevailing Canadian wage . <p> So , are Canadian wages really " too high " ? <p> The reality is that the pay of most workers has stagnated in real terms over the past 30 years as the profit share of GDP has increased at the expense of wages , and as wages have become much more unequal with more and more of the total wage and salary bill going to the top 1 per cent made up mainly of senior executives . As the OECD recently reported , since 1990 , 6 per cent of total national income has been shifted from wages to profits and the pay of the top 1 per cent combined . <p> And a recent Statistics Canada study documents the stagnation of real hourly wages of the majority of Canadian workers between 1981 and 2011 . Over that entire 30-year period , the average hourly wage of full-time workers rose by just 14 per @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) . This compares to growth of over 50 per cent in real GDP per person over the same period . <p> The view that wages are " too high " boils down to saying that workers have no right to share in rising national income , all of which should go to profits and senior managers . That is , to say the least , a curious basis on which to sell the proposition that workers have any kind of stake in our current economic arrangements . <p> Even within the very narrow logic of international economic competitiveness , it is hard to argue that wages put Canadian employers at a significant competitive disadvantage . <p> The U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics publish harmonized data on unit labour costs in manufacturing . These will go up if increases in nominal hourly wages outstrip productivity gains , and are widely used as a measure of competitiveness . The base year is 2002 . <p> Between 2002 and 2010 , Canadian unit labour costs rose by 67.6 per cent in U.S. dollar terms , while U.S. unit labour costs fell by 10.8 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ compared to the U.S. and countries like China , which more or less peg to the U.S. dollar . This readily explains why we lost over 500,000 manufacturing jobs over that period . <p> However , measured in Canadian dollar terms , Canadian unit labour costs rose by only 9.9 per cent , 2002 to 2010 . The appreciation of the Canadian dollar against the U.S. dollar has obviously been the key factor behind loss of competitiveness and jobs . ( Minister Flaherty seemed singularly untroubled by this heading up to his retreat . ) <p> Lagging productivity has also been a significant factor . Between 2002 and 2010 , output per hour in U.S. manufacturing rose by 47.1 per cent compared to a meagre 10.0 per cent in Canada . Minister Flaherty should be asking the CEOs at his retreat why they are sitting on record amounts of retained profits rather than investing in productivity-enhancing machinery and equipment , research and development and worker skills . <p> And what about hourly wages in manufacturing in Canada ? According to the BLS , they rose by just 20.9 per cent , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Bear in mind that these are nominal wage increases . The BLS calculate that annual earnings in manufacturing adjusted for inflation rose by just 1.5 per cent in Canada , 2002-10 , compared to 10.2 per cent in the U.S. <p> So , Canada 's massive loss of competitiveness against the U.S. and countries that peg to the U.S. dollar is overwhelmingly explained by the change in the exchange rate and our appalling productivity record , not by wages . <p> Not that this will stop the CEOs at this year 's retreat from complaining about overpaid Canadian workers . <p> ( Full disclosure . I was invited to and attended the retreat in 2009 . This should ensure that I am not asked back. )

Sierra Club Canada was established in Canada in 1963 and today has five chapters across the country ( BC , Prairie , Ontario , Quebec &amp; Atlantic Canada ) . Sierra Club Canada also has dozens of local groups across the country in communities from Cape Breton to Vancouver ! It also has an amazing youth affiliate -- The Sierra Youth Coalition . Major national campaigns include climate change , nuclear energy , protecting biodiversity and transitioning to a sustainable economy . Sierra Club Canada has earned an excellent reputation within its constituency and the broader public for its thoroughly researched positions and for refusing to compromise on its ideals . Sierra Club Canada believes in the power of people everywhere working @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Let 's study the health impact of the tar sands too ! <p> The federal government recently surprised many when Health Canada announced a $1.8-million study on health impacts of living near wind turbines . Electricity generation and health are both largely provincial responsibilities -- so this is major deviation by a government that has strictly avoided straying into areas of provincial jurisdiction . <p> Could the study have been prompted by the Conservative 's wish to capitalize on the unpopularity of turbines in some rural Ontario ridings , in the process helping the province 's official opposition , Tim Hudak 's Conservatives ? A two-for-one . Scarce health resources being misused for clearly partisan reasons ; it 's all about politics ( you can read more on this in a recent blog of mine ) . <p> Although we should be used to this kind of behavior from Ottawa these days , it 's still startling to see something so blatantly partisan get such little attention ( Bev Oda 's $16 glass of orange juice received a hundred times more coverage ! ) . <p> This million-dollar inquiry , into @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , also strikes me as a bit ironic . Governments usually draw on internationally accepted peer-reviewed scientific literature as a reason to not waste tax-dollars on duplicitous research . <p> In 2011 , Sierra Club Canada did a review of the existing published , peer-reviewed studies . Not surprisingly ( wind energy has been safely used in Germany and across Europe for years ) , after thoroughly examining a wealth of empirical international evidence we found properly located turbines do not present a public health risk . So it 's frustrating , and somewhat alarming , to see the amount of misinformation ( if not disinformation ) being spread by AstroTurf wind opposition groups and ( now ) scared landowners . <p> This political trickery , however , may have inadvertently done us a favour . It has set a precedent for commissioning health studies on the impact of industrial development and a precedent for launching studies in response popular demand . <p> When announcing the turbine " study " , Conservative MP Pierre Poilievre , Parliamentary Secretary to the Ministers of Transport , Infrastructure and Communities and Federal Economic Development @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : " Just as the oil sands are subject to strenuous public health regulations based on sound research . " Calls to Health Canada and a search of its website revealed no such evidence . There have been no thorough health studies conducted in northern Alberta to determine health impacts of tar sands development despite the alarming rate of development over the past few decades . Studies commissioned by both the federal and Alberta governments acknowledged environmental monitoring has been " insufficient " . Willful blindness , perhaps ? <p> Sierra Club Canada , noting the new precedents set by Health Canada 's wind announcement , has begun gathering support from other organizations to demand a study on the health effects of tar sands development on Albertans and others living downstream . <p> If you know an organization that might like to get involved or just want to stay informed on the issue , send me an email . 
@@1090137 <p> Michael D'Andrea had no notion that August day just how wrathful the afternoon would turn , how calamitous , what an effect it would have on the city 's future . <p> It was an off-duty day for the director of Toronto 's Water Infrastructure Management , who had planned a pleasant Niagara-on-the-Lake getaway with his wife in what was a hot , dry summer . <p> " We had lunch , we walked by the lake , " D'Andrea recounts of the pretty afternoon . And then came the reckoning : " I looked toward Toronto and said , ' Oh my God . ' " <p> It seems insufficient now to say a dark cloud loomed , but that 's what D'Andrea remembers . Or not so much a cloud as a vast , menacing mass poised over the top of the city and leaching across the horizon . Driving home to Toronto , the water expert listened to radio reports of massive flooding . " The whole city sounded like it was under water . " <p> That day , Aug. 19 , 2005 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the span of three hours , 153 millimetres of rain fell , a 78-metre stretch of Finch Avenue W. cratered , the Black Creek culvert collapsed , and a 48-metre section of trunk sanitary sewer was lost , spewing raw , uncontrolled sewage into Highland Creek for three days . The basement flooding in private residences was unprecedented . <p> The financial tally for the Finch Avenue Washout ranks as the most expensive natural disaster in the province 's history . The private insurance payout nudges $600 million . As a point of comparison , insurance payouts for the Peterborough flood of 2004 did n't quite reach a sixth of that . <p> We knew it was bad then . <p> Now we know it 's only going to get worse . <p> Between the years 1986 and 2006 , Toronto experienced not one but eight storms of the magnitude that had been predicted to occur no more than once in a quarter-century . The Finch Avenue Washout was the capper , a one-in-100-years storm for which the city 's infrastructure was woefully under-designed . " It was the transformative event @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " D'Andrea says . <p> When we talk about the city 's " adaptation " strategy to water management in the face of extreme weather , the Finch Avenue Washout is the trigger point . In the wake of past storms , D'Andrea explains , the city upsized sanitary sewer systems commensurate with the size of the storm . <p> " Time marches on and inevitably there 's a storm far greater than the storm that system was adjusted to " he says . " So you can appreciate what the public confidence is as we march forward . " Public confidence , D'Andrea adds , acknowledging the sea of sodden city home owners this summer , is " out the window . " <p> Does it help to know that there are many smart people at work on the problem ? Post-2005 , the city quickly and aggressively amended its Wet Weather Flow Master Plan , which had been adopted in 2003 with a primary focus on the cleanup of the waterfront and stream restoration in the hard-surfaced , urbanized environment . The initial master plan made no provisions for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's priority focus shifted to basement flooding protection , with council adopting a new development standard for the sanitary system to provide for control of up to a one-in-100-year storm . That 's the big ticket , from improvements to existing infrastructure , to surface stormwater management ponds and underground storage tanks . Stormwater ponds have been built in Muirhead Park and Moore Park , by example , and an underground storage tank has gone in at old Sheppard Park . Six more storage facilities are planned for construction within the next five years . <p> Green initiatives have been aimed at homeowners which , compounded , could reap significant positive effects : compulsory downspout disconnection to reduce storm water runoff ; the installation of backwater valves within a home 's storm drainage system to prevent basement sewer backup . <p> Under the Ontario Building Code , backwater valves are now mandatory for all new residential construction and are required when renovation work on existing homes involves major work to the drainage system . The city 's basement flooding protection subsidy program for homeowners offers financial assistance to a maximum of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ D'Andrea expresses frustration at those who seek exemptions to allow for the construction of rear slope driveways . ( " Developers may get exemptions , but it 's the property owner who will bear the brunt of the consequences should the system fail , " he says . ) <p> Robert Sandford , chair of the Canadian Partnership Initiative of the United Nations Water for Life Decade , says Toronto 's aggressive posture has put it out in front of other Canadian municipalities . " The foundation of adaptation , and one of the most important things the city of Toronto appears to have grasped , is that certainty , stationarity , no longer exists , " he says , defining " stationarity " as the fluctuation of hydrological systems within established limits , the very guidelines upon which water-management systems have been traditionally designed . <p> " Toronto held up that particular storm event ( Finch Avenue ) and said , ' We think this is the beginning of a serious concern and threat . ' That really turned the attention of other municipalities to the problem that has manifested @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Submitted last December , the SENES outlook predicts fewer yet more violent storms with greater extreme rainfall ( SENES 's precipitation summary for 2040-49 forecasts a doubling of rainfall in the month of August and a more than doubling of precipitation in July . ) <p> " We wanted to get a better understanding of the weather patterns , in particular the extreme weather events that we are likely going to be experiencing as a result of climate change for the Toronto area through the period 2050 , " says TEO director Lawson Oates . " If we generally look at , you know , there 'll be an average one degree increase Celsius , people have the sense well , that 's manageable . But if you look at an extreme event , that 's where the real concern is , both the infrastructure side and also the impacts on vulnerable populations . " <p> When Robert Sandford considers a one degree @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the mid-19th century by a German physicist and a French railway engineer . You could actually model how much atmospheric moisture there will be in a warmer atmosphere . " <p> Here 's another bit of modelling . Michael D'Andrea flips to an orthophotographic image of the Finch Avenue disaster . A digital elevation model of surface flooding reveals a nasty picture : the most damaged homes sit at a notably lower elevation . " I call it the soup bowl effect , " he says , before stating the obvious . " In rampant post-Second World War development they were not really thinking about drainage . " Past generations paid for infrastructure that we 've benefited from over time , but we did n't invest in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ D'Andrea says . " <p> Six years ago , a detailed engineering review was recommended for what D'Andrea 's group identified as 32 chronic basement flooding areas in the city . " We know that as we march forward with those environmental assessments . . . projects are going to cue up and each one with significant financial needs , " he says . <p> The cost for all 32 , work that will largely involve storm sewer upgrades and replacements , is still being tallied . " <p> The trick , of course , is that no true adaptation strategy can ever be fixed in time . " I know we 're designing to an extreme condition without question , " D'Andrea says . @@1090163 <p> rabble blogs are the personal pages of some of Canada 's most insightful progressive activists and commentators . We welcome new bloggers -- contact us for details . <h> Christopher Majka <p> Christopher Majka studied at Mount Alison and Dalhousie Universities , the Pushkin Institute in Moscow and was a guest researcher at the Edward Gray Institute at Oxford University . He has written extensively for many national and international publications . His scientific work includes over 150 scientific papers and contributions to nine books . He is a review editor for two international publications , a collaborator of the Kaltenbach Lab and director of Democracy : Vox Populi . Majka was a recipient of the Tom Brydges Award from the Ecological Monitoring and Assessment Network , and was included as one of Canadian Geographic 's Environmental Scientists of the Year in 2010 . <h> Berlin wallet : Merkel invests , Harper divests <p> I recently ran across the following article : " Evaluation of the rate constant for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fluoride in the gas phase . " Sound familiar ? Perhaps slightly arcane for the general reader ? The lead author ? Angela Merkel -- yes , that Angela Merkel . Merkel is not only the Chancellor of Germany , the de facto leader of the European Union and according to the Daily Telegraph ( London ) , " the most powerful woman in the world , " she is also a whip-smart Ph.D . quantum chemist with a sheaf of scientific publications under her professional belt . <p> The connection between the two -- academic chops and political success -- is not simply coincidental . That 's because Merkel is more than clever -- she 's intelligent and smart . Quantum chemistry is no discipline for intellectual slackers . Applying quantum mechanics to theoretical and experimental chemistry is a highly demanding discipline involving immense creativity , an intuitive grasp of complex and abstract ideas , great computational abilities , and the talent to unravel , understand , and creatively tinker with highly complicated physio-chemical and theoretical systems . In other words , it 's a superb basis for developing an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Renewable energy : German benchmarks <p> One of the defining features of Merkel 's 22-year political career -- from 1994 as Minister for the Environment , Nature Conservation , and Nuclear Safety , then in 1998 as the secretary-general of the Christian Democratic Union ( CDU ) , and since 2005 as German Chancellor -- has been the dramatic development of renewable energy . Germany has been called the " world 's first major renewable energy economy " and the German Renewable Energy Act ( EEG in German ) , which has provided the political springboard for this development , was called " one of the greatest paradigm shifts since the start of the industrial revolution " by the Green Energy Act Alliance . <p> There are three core principles : <p> 1 . Guaranteed long-term ( 20 years ) feed-in tariffs ( specific to each energy source ) for the production of renewable energy , along with a connection requirement . Distribution companies must provide grid connectivity and purchase such renewable power ( whether generated by municipalities , coops , small or medium-sized enterprises , or indeed , individual land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ produced by conventional non-renewable sources ) feed it into the transmission and distribution grid . <p> 2 . There is no public cost or subsidy . The feed-in tariffs are paid by electricity distributors to electricity producers and recouped from the rates they charge to consumers . Thus consumers who are most thrifty and conservation-minded pay less than their energy profligate peers -- and this applies both to individuals as well as enterprises . Moreover , in contrast to fossil fuels , whose use produces environmental , climatic , and human health impacts , the production of electricity from renewable sources has far fewer externalized costs , thus saving the state in pollution and health remediation costs . <p> 3 . Innovation and efficiency are promoted by a periodic decrease of feed-in tariffs for new developments ( i.e. , a degression of 1 per cent per year ) . <p> These measure have produced astonishing economic , technological , and environmental results . The figure below shows the generation of renewable electricity ( in Gigawatt hours , GWh ) between 1990 and 2011 . Hydro-electric generation has remained relatively constant but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ since the EEG was introduced in 2000 Note : production of geothermal energy really only began in 2004 and stayed minuscule until 2008 . It has increased to 18.8 GWh , but this is still a tiny sliver compared to other sources and so it does n't show on the figure below . <p> To place this in an overall electricity context , the figure below shows the proportion of renewable and non-renewable electric generation capacity in Megawatts over this same time period . In 1990 the renewable fraction was only 3.2 per cent , which increased only to 5.7 per cent by the end of the decade . Since then , however , the renewable fraction has almost quintupled to 25.2 per cent -- a dramatic rate of growth . Moreover , Germany has set ambitious goals for the future , namely to produce 35 per cent of its electricity from renewable energy by 2020 , and 80 per cent by 2050 . This is a phenomenal objective , particularly given that Germany committed to closing all of its nuclear reactors in 2011 following the Fukashima nuclear disaster . Eight @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ down by 2022 ( Germany once generated 150,000 GWh of electricity from nuclear sources ) . <p> Germany : Environmental success <p> Concurrent with this development of renewable energy , Germany is also making significant strides in energy efficiency , the goal being to cut national electrical consumption to 50 per cent below 2008 levels by 2050 . <p> Not only has there been a dramatic increase in the development of all these renewable energy sources and in energy conservation , these changes have manifested themselves on the environmental front . In 1990 ( the base year for the Kyoto protocol ) Germany emitted 1,246.1 Megatonnes ( Mt ) of carbon dioxide equivalent greenhouse gases ( GHG ) . By 2000 this had decreased to 1,039 Mt and by 2011 it was down to 916.7 Mt , a 26.5 per cent reduction . Under the Kyoto Protocol , Germany was obliged to decrease its GHG emissions 21 per cent from 1990 levels by 2012 , an objective that it met in 2008 , four years ahead of schedule . On the employment front , some 340,000 people are employed in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the workforce . <p> " The German government has chosen the socio-ecological market economy as the framework for shaping production and consumption in keeping with sustainable development , while at the same time encouraging innovation in industry and society . The key is to sever the traditional link between economic growth and the consumption of resources , which increasingly threatens the natural basis for life and the preservation of natural and landscape diversity . <p> " Technical and scientific innovations provide excellent prospects for environmental protection . It is vital that we use our growing knowledge and capabilities responsibly , and that we use them in the interest of environmentally appropriate development . Science must play an important role in the pursuit of sustainable development . " <p> The Canadian record <p> It is worth comparing these benchmarks to Canadian ones . <p> In terms of renewable energy , Canada has an enormous advantage over Germany as a result of our extensive hydro resources , which in 2011 generated 372,780 GWh , almost 63 per cent of Canadian electricity . Wind generated 7,560 GWh , 1.3 per cent , while other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and geothermal ) comprise less than 0.5 per cent . While 64.6 per cent of Canadian electricity is generated from renewable sources , 35.4 per cent still comes from non-renewable sources , chiefly nuclear ( 15.2 per cent ) and fossil fuels ( 20.2 per cent ) including coal , coke , petroleum , light and heavy oil , diesel , and natural gas . <p> Thus , although in absolute proportions Canada is much further ahead of Germany ( 64.6 per cent versus 25.2 per cent of electricity from renewable sources ) , the reality is Canada is making almost no progress . In 1990 Canada generated 61.5 percent of electricity from renewable sources ( almost all from hydro ) , so over 21 years we have added only 3.1 per cent to our renewable energy output share while over the same time period Germany added 22 per cent to its , the lion 's share in the last decade . <p> Furthermore , while Germany committed to reducing its GHG emissions 21 per cent below 1990 levels by 2012 under the Kyoto Protocols ( a goal which it achieved @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them by 25.6 per cent . Canada , on the other hand , committed to reducing its GHG emissions by 6 per cent from 1990 levels ( a quarter of Germany 's proportionate decrease ) by 2012 . How has Canada fared ? In 2010 our emissions were actually 17.5 per cent above 1990 levels . While Germany had to do almost four times the heavy lifting of Canada -- and it succeeded -- Canada failed to act and its GHG emissions ballooned . Then the Harper Conservatives pulled the plug on the Kyoto Protocol altogether as part of the myriad provisions of Bill C-38 , the infamous omnibus budget bill of June 2012 . <p> In terms of employment , numbers for the renewable energy sector in Canada are difficult to find , however a report by the Canadian Centre for Policy Alternatives , Climate Change and the Canadian Energy Sector by John Calvert and Marjorie Griffin Cohen , estimates that every 10 Megawatts of installed capacity results in approximately 6 jobs . Glenn Lowson photo for Postmedia -- Reporter Margaret Munro <p> TORONTO -- Sheila Colla wades into the pye weeds , her net swooping over the pink flowers . " This is bumble bee heaven , " she says , as she catches a bee and nudges it from the net into a small plastic vial for inspection . <p> It 's a lemon cuckoo bumble bee , one of the strange cast of characters in the bee world . The cuckoo bee invades colonies , usurps the queen and enslaves her workers . <p> There is no sign , however , of the rusty-patched bumble bee that Colla has been searching for all summer . It used to be one of the most common bees in southern Ontario and Quebec but is now one of the rarest . <p> In fact , the rusty-patched bumble bee , known to scientists as Bombus affinis , is the first bee in North America to be officially declared an endangered species . <p> Federal Environment Minister Peter Kent added the bee to Canada 's list @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be on the brink of extinction . <p> Climate change , pesticides , habitat loss and pathogens are suspects , but no one can say with certainty why it has vanished . <p> Few people even realized the bee was in trouble until Colla , a PhD student at York University , documented the drastic decline . " It shows we are really not paying attention , which is kind of scary , " she says . <p> Bees may not be everyone 's favourite bugs -- onlookers tend to take a few steps back when they realize Colla is using her net to catch bees , not butterflies -- but they are key ecological players . <p> They 're essential for production of fruit and seeds that feed everything from bears to birds . They also pollinate crops that produce one-third of the human food supply . <p> " Yet most people consider them to be an irritant , " says York biologist Laurence Packer , keeper of Canada 's biggest bee collection . <p> The cabinets in his lab contain thousands of bees from as far north as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are the size of aphids , others big as small birds . There are also dozens of different species of bumble bees , which Packer describes as " the charismatic fauna of the bee world . " <p> The only species the York team avoids is the honey bee , which gets plenty of attention from other researchers . <p> Packer and his graduate students focus on wild bees -- about 19,500 species in the world , 808 of them in Canada . They say the insects deserve more attention and appreciation , in part because they are such sensitive environmental indicators . " They 're like the canary in the coal mine , " says Packer . <p> And when a common species such as the rusty-patched bumble bee disappears , he says , " it 's a warning signal that things are going wrong in the great outdoors . " <p> Many bees across Canada are in decline . The western bumble bee has vanished from most of its range in British Columbia and the United States in the last decade , and Colla says that about half the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ population drops in recent decades . <p> But the loss of the rusty-patched bumble bee has been most dramatic . <p> The bee with a distinct orange patch on its abdomen used to forage for pollen and nectar on milkweeds , sunflowers , clovers , berry and fruit blossoms across Southern Ontario and Quebec . <p> Colla became intrigued in 2004 when she came across a survey from the 1970s of 3,632 bees from southern Ontario . Bombus affinis was the fourth most common of 14 species . <p> She repeated the survey and found not a single rusty-patched bumble bee . Colla expanded the search to other areas of Ontario and the U.S. She did eventually find two in Pinery Provincial Park in southwest Ontario : one in 2005 , the other in 2009 . The bees are now skewered on straight pins and stored in a glass-covered case in the York lab . <p> Only one other rusty-patched bee has been spotted in Canada in the last decade . It , too , was seen in Pinery Park in 2009 . <p> The search for the bee continues this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of sites where the Bombus affinis was historically found in Ontario for a project sponsored by Wildlife Preservation Canada , a non-profit group . Colla and Wazbinski are catching plenty of bumble bees -- they netted nine different species one day near Peterborough , Ont. -- but not a single rusty-patched bumble bee . The situation is only slightly better in the U.S. , with researchers finding a few of the bees in Wisconsin and Minnesota this summer . <p> While most people ca n't tell one species from another , bumblebees are not interchangeable . <p> Bombus polaris , which flits around the Arctic pollinating tundra flowers that produce wild berries , has thick pile on its body and can withstand long , dark , sub-zero winters . <p> The rusty-patched bee was the earliest bee to emerge in the spring in southern Canada and one of the last to stop foraging in the fall . It was also adapt at " nectar-robbing , " strategically slicing tiny holes in flowers to get at nectar not otherwise reachable with its short tongue , says Colla , who is working on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bees in North America . <p> Unlike honey bees that overwinter in their colonies , bumble bees live from just spring to fall with only queens surviving the winter . <p> Bumble bee queens emerge in the spring and forage alone for several weeks , replenishing their fat supply " just like a bear , " says Colla . The queen then makes a nest , often in a rodent burrow , and lays eggs to produce 100 to 200 workers over the summer to take care of the nest and forage for food . In late summer " new queens and kings " are produced and mate , says Colla . Fertilized new queens go into hibernation while the rest of the bees perish . <p> Worker bumble bees are common in the summer months and are not much bigger than honey bees . They are usually a combination of black , orange and yellow , unlike honey bees that have brownish bodies , says Colla , who can identify the different species at a glance . <p> She is also quick to spot the males , which do n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The seemingly dazed male bee meanders across her palm and eventually takes flight . <p> Colla and Wazbinski may spend their time catching bees , but they have yet to be stung this summer . They are more concerned about ticks , poison ivy and thorny plants . " Walking into a field of thistle is no fun , " says Colla . <p> The demise of the rusty-patched bumble bee has been linked to several factors , but the mystery is far from solved . <p> One suspect is pathogens spread by commercial greenhouse operations that use bumble bees to pollinate tomatoes , cucumbers and other hothouse crops . The commercial bees are allowed to fly in an out of Canadian greenhouses , says Colla , who would like Canada 's hothouse industry to explore keeping its bees inside with screens , as has been proposed in Japan . <p> Potent pesticides , called neonicotinoids , which are widely used to kill bugs on crops and fleas on domestic pets , may also be associated with the demise of Bombus affinis . Recent European studies suggest the chemicals interfere @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ back to their nests . <p> Colla and researcher Jeremy Kerr at the University of Ottawa are looking into the possibility that climate change may have hit the rusty-patched bee hard . <p> Because the bee emerged so early in the spring and foraged until late fall , it was vulnerable to storms and weather extremes that have grown more pronounced in recent decades , says Colla . <p> Scientists say the loss of the bee may be impacting food chains and ecosystems . Bombus affinis was known to visit everything from raspberries and lilacs in the spring to goldenrod and asters in the fall . But they say it is difficult to pin down effects . " I worry about the spring ephemeral flowers , " says Colla , noting the rusty-patched bumble bee was one of the few insects around to pollinate trilliums , trout lilies and spring lilies . <p> Packer says things are amiss when a species vanishes . And he says human society needs to become more attuned to the changes underway in the natural world . <p> In his book " Keeping the Bees , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risk from habitat loss , pesticide use , disease and other factors . Because of the integral role the insects play in the ecology of the planet , he warns " we may be at risk as well . " " It 's the small things that run the world . @@1091603 <p> There 's a post-rain freshness at the Kortright Centre for Conservation as Glenn MacMillan tamps his boots across the still-wet pavers . <p> MacMillan is senior manager , water and energy , for the Toronto and Region Conservation Authority , and he 's based here at Kortright , the city 's bucolic testing ground for energy and conservation initiatives . <p> The pavers in question are deemed pervious , or permeable , due to the void space that rims the hard stones , space that is filled with penetrable , pea-sized aggregate . So conceptually , a storm blows through and the storm water infiltrates at the point of contact , as opposed to the runoff that currently overtaxes the city 's sewers . <p> " It 's the greening of infrastructure , " says MacMillan . " Part of the driver for it is climate change adaptation . So you 're building resiliency into your storm water system . . . a series of measures to manage storm water the way it flowed naturally . " <p> A side-by-side study underway at the Kortright is examining @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from driveways to parking lots to alleyways . Different pavers with differently sized void spaces are being tested , as is pervious concrete , which is poured in place and allows water to infiltrate throughout , and a product that looks like asphalt but is composed of ground recycled tires . <p> Progressive ? Yes . The first test area was installed in 2005 , just two years after the city of Chicago launched its Green Alley program , promoting permeable pavement as well as the regrading of alleyways to drain away from private property . <p> In Seattle , a new housing development called High Point featured two permeable pavement tests , the first dating back to 2004 . One test is a one-block road of pervious concrete . " It 's really interesting material because you 're trying to optimize the void space that lets water through while trying to maintain the strength , which means less void space , " says Timothy Lowry , senior civil engineer with Seattle Public Utilities . " The testing we use for conventional concrete suggested it was n't strong , it was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , that road has held up quite well for coming on eight years . " <p> The news is good , but cautionary . " We have roads in Seattle that are 75 years old and holding up quite well , " says Maureen Meehan , storm water strategic adviser with Seattle 's Department of Transportation . " The cost of replacement is high . There 's been no permeable pavement in place for long enough to really know ( how it will hold up ) . " <p> The long-term maintenance cost remains an unknown . Lowry emphasizes that the craftsmen 's skill is also an issue -- contractors like to give the concrete a nice smooth finish which , as Lowry says , " Looks real pretty but it does n't function . " Seattle is further constrained by utilities placed in the road right of way . " Infiltrating water around existing utilities would cause a lot of problems , " Meehan notes . <p> All that said , Seattle is targeting greenfield developments as well as permeable sidewalks , both of which offer two of the greatest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pavement in the north end of the city that is just about complete . <p> " Where we are ahead of the curve is assessing how this technology mitigates storm water flow control and trying to implement it instead of a detention pond , " says Meehan . Adds Lowry : " The whole storm water industry is going to need to provide resiliency if the storms become more intense . That 's my understanding , that we will have shorter periods of more rain . " <p> At the Kortright Centre , Jennifer Drake , a Ph.D . student at the University of Guelph , has focused her research on the performance of permeable pavements . In an email , she documents the environmental benefits : " This infiltrated water will have different characteristics than runoff produced from traditional , impervious pavements . . . smaller total volumes , slower peak flow rates and longer drainage times . In addition , infiltrating storm water through permeable pavement systems improves the overall water quality by removing many common pollutants and by mitigating excessively warm water temperatures during summer months that otherwise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> All good , when it works . Glenn MacMillan points to part of a test area that has experienced clogging and pooling around the hard stone pavers . An Elgin sweeper , an industrial vacuum , was brought in to suck up the top inch or so of the bedding material , which is then replaced with fresh material . " Maintenance is one of the big issues we 're dealing with here . How do you maintain this type of technology ? " When the void spaces are functioning as designed , as much as 99 per cent of the rainfall has been shown to infiltrate the pavement . Is the maintenance on porous concrete less demanding ? " That 's something we 're going to be determining , " he says . <p> MacMillan is working with the American Society of Civil Engineers with a goal to standardize the design , construction and maintenance of permeable pavement . In the meantime , an operation maintenance guide is being drawn up to provide guidance to municipalities . Developers are being offered demonstrations on the benefits of going green @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Need a new walkway ? A driveway ? " You could sell it as an upgrade , " MacMillan says shrewdly . Granite countertops are yesterday . The future : pervious pavers.

@@1102251 <h> The World Health Organization says the disease behind " Black Death " is still widespread in many countries in Africa , the former Soviet Union , Asia and the Americas . <p> To better understand the modern plague bacterium and its global distribution , researchers continue to monitor the disease 's main carriers -- rodents -- and changes in both climate and human development patterns that may affect plague-carrying rodent populations . <p> By:Niamh ScallanStaff Reporter , Published on Sun Aug 26 2012 <p> One of the deadliest pathogens in human history , the " Black Death " plague wiped out more than 25 million people after landing on Europe 's doorstep in the late 1340s . <p> Though often seen as a problem of the past , plague remains a major threat to human health in some areas of the world , with between 1,000 and 2,500 cases reported globally every year . <p> According to the World Health Organization , the disease is widespread in many countries in Africa , the former Soviet Union , Asia and the Americas and " the mortality due to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the modern plague bacterium and its global distribution , researchers continue to monitor the disease 's main carriers -- rodents -- and changes in both climate and human development patterns that may affect plague-carrying rodent populations . <p> THE DISEASE <p> Caused by the Yersinia pestis bacterium , plague is most often carried by flea-infested rodents and transmitted to humans via flea bite . The infection usually begins with flu-like symptoms before taking one of three forms . <p> Bubonic plague is the most common type of the disease , an infection of the lymph nodes that can be treated with antibiotics if caught early . If left untreated , the bacterial infection kills about half of those it infects . <p> Septicemic plague , which spreads through the bloodstream , is transmitted through either flea bites or human-to-human infection . <p> Pneumonic plague , the final and most infectious form of the disease , affects the lungs and can be transmitted through the air . <p> Believed to have originated in Central Asia , plague spread to other continents via shipping routes . In the late 19th century , a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ North America when it sailed from Asia to the U.S. west coast . <p> " That was really the introduction of the plague bacteria to the United States , " said Dr. Hendrik Poinar , an evolutionary geneticist at the Ancient DNA Centre at Hamilton 's McMaster University . <p> From there , the bacterium slowly spread throughout the southwestern United States through wild animals native to the area ( rats , squirrels , prairie dogs etc . ) . Today , cases of plague are most prevalent in the southwest and Pacific regions of the U.S. <p> " The rodents that are the good carrier population are mostly found in the southwest . . . and the Rockies prevent the movement of these little guys , " said Poinar , explaining why plague cases have mostly remained clustered in the southwest . <p> CLIMATE AND DEVELOPMENT <p> While much remains unknown about plague , a 2012 U.S. National Wildlife Health Center report noted a number of factors that may contribute to its spread . According to the report , climate change has been shown to have varying effects on plague @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ down plague cases , while increased rainfall boosts the number ) . <p> Humans also put themselves in the way of the bacterium , said the report , by expanding housing developments or taking part in recreational activities such as camping in areas notorious for the plague bacterium . <p> Dr. Frederick Leighton , executive director of the Canadian Cooperative Wildlife Health Centre , said the number of plague cases in Canada remains low , with the last recorded human plague case dating back to 1939 . <p> Some parts of Canada 's dry prairie lands , however , have proven to be " appropriate habitats " for plague-carrying rodents and the sprawling urban communities expanding into the foothills of the Canadian Rockies are areas of risk , he said . <p> " It would be wrong to say it 's terrible and lurking , but we know it 's there , " Leighton said . " We should be vigilant for it , particular as population increases and land use changes . Plague , like many other things , will increase in risk if we engage in risky behaviour . @
